**[00:08:26.670] Sen. Francis "Chiz" G. Escudero**  
The justice system cannot function effectively when the professionals charged with administering it cannot even be polite to one another. The Chair understands that patience is wearing thin, tensions are running high, and tempers are beginning to fray. But we must not lose sight of our common purpose. Our duty is not to prevail over one another but to assist this Court in the fair and orderly administration of justice. This reminder applies not only to counsel present before this Court, under the brightest spotlight on the largest stage, but to every lawyer beyond this courtroom. Let all be reminded that Canon 2, Section 2 of the CIPRO requires every lawyer to deal with fellow members of the Bar with courtesy, civility, fairness, and candor. To non-lawyers, Justice Kennedy said it more aptly, quote,

**[00:09:25.190] Sen. Francis "Chiz" G. Escudero**  
but it only survives in civility. That responsibility belongs to everyone touched by these proceedings. Counsel, non-counsels alike, parties, non-parties alike, witnesses and putative witnesses alike, public officials, commentators, and observers alike. Disagreement may be vigorous and criticism unsparing, but no cause is strengthened by contempt. No institution is preserved by conduct that strips another person of his or her dignity. Our duty is not to conquer one another in these proceedings, but to eliminate the truth and serve the cause of justice. Let the arguments be forceful but never personal. Let the contest be exacting but never degrading. And when these proceedings are concluded, let it be the law Not anger, not pride, nor applause. Be the final word. Kinikilala po ng presiding officer na sa yugtoy ito ng ating paglitis, medyo maikli na ang pasensya ng karamihan, medyo mataas ang emosyon ng ilan, at medyo naguumapaw ang galit, puot, at inis. Bugso ng anumang nakikita o napapanood nila sa mga pagdinig na ito. Subalit, hindi tayo pwede mawala ng loob sa ating ginagampanan tungkulin. Hindi tayo pwede.

Thank you, Your Honor. Your Honor, we are offering the testimony of Atty. Ronel U. Benaventura to prove the following. Number one, billions worth of transactions passed through the accounts of the respondent and her husband. Mula 2007 hanggang 2025, 4.4 billion ang iniulat sa AMLC. Ito ang kabuahang halaga ng mga transaksyon ni Navipi Sara at Atty. Manasseh Scorpio. Iniulat ang mga ito ng mga bangko at iba pang covered persons dahil malaki o kahina-hinala. Hindi pa ito ang lahat ng dumaan sa kanilang mga account.

**[00:26:59.920] Atty. Mae Sancho Divinagracia**  
At least 1.6 billion came in. At least 1.3 billion went out. And no record shows where it went. May 1.6 billion na natukoy na pumasok sa mga account ng mag-asawa. At 1.3 billion naman ang natukoy na lumabas. 1.4 billion pa ang hindi matukoy kung pumasok o lumabas. At ang mga ito ay base pa lamang sa mga CTRs at STRs na isinumiti sa AMLC. Samantala, walang cash na idineklara sa kanyang SALN para sa 2020 hanggang 2024. At sa mga lumabas, wala nang bakas kung saan ito napunta. Number 3. Even after a bank corrected its own error, 4.4 billion in aggregate transactions remained. 6.77 billion ang iniulat ng AMLC sa House noong Abril 2026. Sa kabila ng pagtatama ng BPI sa diumanoy sarili nitong pagkakamali sa sistema, 4.4 billion suspicious at covered transactions pa rin ang natitira. Number four, her husband walked out of two banks with 41 million in cash in a single day. Sa loob lamang ng isang araw noong Augusto 6, 2024, 41 million ang kinuha na cash ni Atty. Carpio, 23 million sa BDO at 18 million sa PNB. Pero sa kanyang 2024 SALN, walang cash na idineklara. No. 5 A person allegedly linked to illegal drugs bought checks naming the respondent as beneficiary. Noong 2011 at 2012, Bumili si Samuel Kang Uy, isang taong diumanoy may kaugnayan sa iligal na droga, at ang kanyang mga anak ng mga tseke na si VP Sara ang binipisyaryo. 14,881,692 pesos and 14 centavos ang kabuuhang halaga. Sila ang naglabas ng pera pero si VP Sara ang tumanggap nito. Number 6. Banks and insurers flagged them again. Flag them again and again. 34 na suspicious transaction report ang nasa file ng respondent, 27 derogatory report ang isinampa tungkol sa kanya, at 70 naman tungkol kay Atty. Carpio. Ang mga dahilan, graft and corrupt practices at malversation of public funds, posibleng kaugnayan sa iligal na droga, at mga transaksyong konektado sa anomalya sa flood control. Hindi ito iisang alarma. Paulit-ulit itong babala mula sa iba't ibang institusyon sa loob ng maraming taon.

**[00:30:00.000] Atty. Mae Sancho Divinagracia**  
Ang Cale88 Foods Corporation na itinatag lamang noong 2021 at 47.5% ng shares ay hawak ni Atty. Carpio ay tumanggap ng 319 million na remittance mula sa mga kumpanya sa China. Tatlo rito ang iniulat ng bangko bilang kahinahinala. Walang legal o trade obligation. Walang layunin at walang katwirang pang-ekonomiya. Number eight, their companies moved billions more. Mula 2022 hanggang 2025, halos 4.5 billion ang kabuuhang pumasok at lumabas sa 15 korporasyon kung saan may interes ang mag-asawa. Iba pa ito sa 44.4 billion na pumasok at lumabas sa accounts ng mag-asawa. Number 9. Every figure comes from the bank's own reports. Ang lahat ng ito ay galing sa sariling ulat ng mga banko at insurer na tinanggap, itinago, at isinumiti ng AMLC sa ukumang ito noong July 30, 2026. Number 10. Banks and insurers report these transactions because this is how dirty money moves. Inaatasan ng bata sa mga bangko, insurer at iba pang covered person na

**[00:31:26.600] Atty. Mae Sancho Divinagracia**  
i-report sa AMLC ang malalaking transaction sa cash at mga transaksyong kahinahinala dahil dito karaniwang dumadaan ang perang galing sa iligal na gawain. Such other matters relevant to the instant impeachment case, including the powers and functions of the AMLC, the laws and rules on covered and suspicious transactions, and the procedures the AMLC followed in complying with the subpoenas of this honorable court.

**[00:31:55.320] Sen. Francis "Chiz" G. Escudero**  
Attorney Mark?

**[00:31:57.700] Atty. Mark C. Vinluan**  
Comments, Your Honor.

**[00:32:03.170] Atty. Mark C. Vinluan**  
First, Rule 128, Section 3 of the Rules of Court provides that evidence is admissible when it is relevant to the issue and is not excluded by the Constitution, the law, or these rules. Therefore, Evidence to be introduced must be competent, and the test of that competence is whether the Constitution, the law, or the rules allow it. Second, under Section 8A of the Anti-Money Laundering Act or AMLA of 2001, which took effect on February 8, 2021, the Anti-Money Laundering Council or AMLC and its Secretariat shall securely protect information received or processed and shall not reveal in any manner any information known to them by reason of their office. This prohibition is absolute and applies to Executive Director-Attorney Ronel U. Buenaventura today and even after his separation from the AMLC. Under this provision and the current state of the law, impeachment is not an exception. Any reference to past impeachment cases, including the Corona impeachment, is therefore unavailing. Number three, it is thus clear that the law prohibits the disclosure in any manner of confidential information, including those submitted by the banks, and other covered persons to the AMLC. The law explicitly extends this prohibition to the members of the media which are here today for the purpose of broadcasting these proceedings to the general public.

**[00:34:09.240] Atty. Mark C. Vinluan**  
Number four, even the AMLC itself warns about the use of its reports such as prosecution exhibit P-2-7-15-B-AMLC stating that the end user of the information must number one verify the information provided number two protect the confidentiality of the information number three use the information for intelligence purposes only number four not use the information as evidence in formal proceedings and number five Refrain from reproducing, copying, extracting, printing, or distributing material to any third party without the written consent of the AMLC. The defense therefore respectfully submits that allowing this witness to testify will breach confidentiality, violate the law, and disregard the explicit warning by the AMLC. all in violation of the respondent accused rights. We therefore put on record the defense is not and will not be complicit in breaking the law. Lastly, allowing the disclosure of confidential information today sets a dangerous precedent because bank transaction records of the President, Senators, members of the House of Representatives, Supreme Court Justices, other government officials or any other Filipino citizen for that matter can and will be susceptible to similar reckless and abusive acts in the future. Based on the foregoing and with all due respect, the defense is compelled to move for the exclusion of this witness. We also put on record that Vice President Sara Z. Duterte reserves the right to pursue all appropriate legal remedies against those who will violate the law and her rights thereunder. Thank you, Your Honor.

Chair will now make a ruling with respect to the motion to exclude by the counsel for the respondent, Attorney Mark.

**[00:41:21.790] Sen. Francis "Chiz" G. Escudero**  
The counsel for the respondent invokes Section 9C and 14D of Republic Act No. 9160 as amended, together with Section A introduced by Republic Act No. 11521. Respondent argues that Republic v.

**[00:41:39.370] Sen. Francis "Chiz" G. Escudero**  
Sandiganbayan was decided under the prior law and therefore may not be controlling anymore. The Court agrees that Section 8A must be considered. However, it does not follow that AMLC reports have thereby become absolutely immune from compulsory process. Section 9C prohibits covered persons and their officers and employees from disclosing that a covered or suspicious transaction report was made, its contents or related information. In Republic v. Sandiganbayan, the Supreme Court held that the prohibition applies to reporting institutions, not to the AMLC itself. Republic Act No. 11521 did not alter Section 9C in that material respect. Thus, Republic v. Sandiganbayan remains controlling on the scope of Section 9C of Republic Act No. 9160. Section 14D as amended imposes penalties for a breach of Section 9C or Section 8A. It does not enlarge those provisions, declare AMLC reports inadmissible, or create an express privilege against a subpoena. Criminal liability attaches only to a prohibited disclosure. Compliance with the lawful order subject to proper safeguards does not constitute to the mind of the court such a breach. Section 8A is the new provision directly governing the AMLC and its Secretariat. It requires them to protect information received or processed and prohibits its unauthorized revelation. But its second paragraph expressly directs the AMLC to establish rules governing information exchange and dissemination. As well as access to such information. The provision therefore regulates the manner and conditions of disclosure. It does not command absolute silence under every circumstance. Sections 8A and 14D are not wholly novel. Section 8A is substantially codified. The confidentiality duties already found in Rule 2, Section 7 of the original AMLA IRR, and later in Rule 22B of the 2016 revised IRR. Sections 9C and 14D, meanwhile, already appeared in the original AMLA with Rule 3, Section 2H of the original IRR, merely implementing the statutory prohibition and penalty for reporting related breaches. Republic Act No. 11521 thus incorporated the pre-existing AMLC confidentiality duty to the statute and brought its violation within Section 14D's existing penal framework, rather than creating an entirely new absolute privilege against lawful compulsory process. This finds support too with the Journal of the Senate during the debates on 11.521, Republic Act No. 11521 wherein the sponsor and author stated that it was merely lifted and codified from the existing AMLA rules and regulations at that time. Section 8A must also be read in pari materia or in relation with Section 7, which retains the expanded AMLC's authority to investigate suspicious transactions, institute civil forfeiture, and other remedial proceedings, cause the filing of complaints before the Department of Justice or the Ombudsman, and resort to judicial processes including subpoenas. Section 11 of Republic Act No. 11521 likewise preserves All provisions of the AMLA not consistent with the amendment. These provisions must be harmonized so that the thing may rather have effect than be destroyed. Section 8A protects AMLC information against leaks, indiscriminate publication, and other unauthorized disclosure. It cannot be construed to disable the AMLC from performing the very investigative and litigation functions that Congress simultaneously preserved or from complying with the lawful and particularized subpoena subject to judicial safeguards. Thus, while Republic v. Sandiganbayan did not interpret the subsequently enacted Section 8A, the reliance on that circumstance is unavailing. The decision remains authoritative as to the unchanged Section 9C and its central reasoning remains applicable. The AMLC is not a passive repository and confidentiality cannot be invoked in a manner that defeats its statutory mandate. Republic Act No. 11521 Strengthened Information Security It did not overrule Republic v. Sandiganbayan nor repeal AMLC's functions or confer immunity form a subpoena. Further, Section 2 of Republic Act No. 1405, as pointed out by Senator-Judge Lacson in our last hearing, separately and expressly permits inquiry into bank deposits in cases of impeachment. The Supreme Court recognized this statutory exception in PNB v. Gancayco, G.R. No. 18343 While this exception does not by itself dispose of the distinct confidentiality imposed on AMLC information, it confirms the policy that bank secrecy is not an absolute bar to the constitutional accountability process. The AMLC witness may therefore identify, authenticate, and explain the report specified in the subpoena. This ruling does not authorize unrestricted public disclosure. Testimony shall be confined only to relevant matters and may, when necessary, be received in camera as the case may be. Either party may still object on grounds of authentication, relevance, hearsay, completeness, accuracy, admissibility, or evidentiary weight, or the presence or absence of personal knowledge.

**[00:47:56.940] Sen. Francis "Chiz" G. Escudero**  
The motion to exclude is denied without prejudice to the counsel who responded, making any objections in the course of the testimony of Attorney Ronel of the AMLC.

**[01:04:23.830] AMLC Executive Director Atty. Ronel U. Buenaventura**

So kasama po dito yung mga banko, remittance company, e-wallet, at iba pa. Yung pangalawa naman po ay mga regulated po ng insurance commission. Kasama po rito yung mga insurance company, pre-need companies, at iba pa po. Yung pangatlo naman po, yung regulated po ng Securities and Exchange Commission kasama po dito yung mga securities broker, securities dealer, investment houses, at iba pa. At yung huling grupo naman po, hindi po sila regulated yung tatlong nabanggit ko pong ahensya pero Nakalagay po sila sa batas. So ang tawag po namin sa kanila ay designated non-financial businesses and professions or DNFBPs. Sila naman po kasama po dito yung mga casinos, jewelry dealers, real estate broker, real estate developers at iba pa po. So sila po yung mga covered persons po natin.

**[01:05:31.460] Atty. Mae Sancho Divinagracia**

**[01:05:36.870] AMLC Executive Director Atty. Ronel U. Buenaventura**  
Your Honor, yung covered transaction is any transaction in cash or monetary instrument in excess of 500,000 pesos in a banking day. Kung ang reporting entity po ay casino, ang halaga po ay 5 million pesos in cash. At kung ang reporting, ang covered person na nagre-report naman po ay Royal estate broker or developer that is 7.5 million in cash po.

**[01:14:46.560] Atty. Mae Sancho Divinagracia**

**[01:14:50.230] Atty. Mae Sancho Divinagracia**  
Ngayon, Atty. Buenaventura, maaari mo ba sabihin kung paano nire-report ang mga covered and suspicious transactions?

**[01:15:01.810] AMLC Executive Director Atty. Ronel U. Buenaventura**  
Covered transactions po, they are reported po based on a filing of report known as covered transaction report or CTRs po. They are prepared by covered persons, one transaction, one report po. Ganun din po yung suspicious transaction po, one report, one suspicious circumstance or transaction, and then one report po. Although yung po STR po kasi, we also, covered persons po also file, hindi lang po based dun sa transaction, pwede din po dun sa based sa particular account, entire account po, if or client relationship, as long as meron pong suspicious circumstance po.

**[01:15:46.930] Atty. Mae Sancho Divinagracia**  
Nabanggit niyo po na yung covered person ay maaaring mag-report ng tungkol sa buong account o client relationship. Paano po ba ito nire-report?

**[01:16:00.970] AMLC Executive Director Atty. Ronel U. Buenaventura**  
Your Honor, yung...

**[01:16:05.320] AMLC Executive Director Atty. Ronel U. Buenaventura**  
Yung per account po, ang treatment po is hindi po transaction-based. Ang nare-report po nila is if they find a suspicion on the entire account or entire account. So yung basis po kasi ng suspicious transaction po is regardless of the amount. So wala pong amount na tinitignan. Ang tinitignan po is kung may suspicion. So kung may suspicion po sila na pasok yung entire account po, they will report it as such po, Your Honor.

**[01:16:34.760] Atty. Mae Sancho Divinagracia**

**[01:16:57.790] Atty. Mae Sancho Divinagracia**

Yung suspicious transaction po, kung yung amount po nung tingin ng covered person po na suspicious, pero kung account-based naman po yung entire account po yung nareport, maaaring pong zero yung ilagay nila kasi hindi naman po talaga transaction-based po yung nareport. Meron din po kami... Na maaari nung araw po kasi yung system po namin may limitation po. So naglalagay po sila ng 1, I think 1 peso minsan po, parang minimal amount kasi nare-reject po. But technically po, wala po talaga. Pag report po yung entire account, hindi po transaction yung nare-report. So typically po, dapat po lumabas zero or wala po, blank po lang po yun kasi account po. Wala pong amount, bali po.

**[03:06:53.210] Sen. Pia S. Cayetano**  
Thank you. Mr. Presiding Officer, Mr. Presiding Officer, I've read the ruling and as a Senator-Judge, I would just like to put on record some observations I have on this ruling.

**[03:07:07.920] Sen. Pia S. Cayetano**  
There are two laws here. The Bank Secrecy Law, RA 1405, as amended, and AMLA Law, RA 9160, as amended. Both cases deal with the utmost confidentiality. of bank records. But there is an exception under the bank secrecy law. And this, it says that in cases of, in impeachment case, this may be divulged. This exception is not found in the AMLA law. And this is where my issue as a Senator-Judge lies. I think we'll all agree as Senator-Judges I would think, I would think, but I cannot presume, but I would think that even the defense and prosecutions, once na matapos na tong kasong to, tapos na, hindi nang maki-question pa yung legality of the proceedings. So when we proceed with an interpretation that the AMLA law now allows for bank records to be presented in the impeachment court, It puts a cloud over the impeachment proceedings, Mr. President. Why is this important? Well, we go back to the Bible, the Bible of our legal, the Bible in the Philippines for our legal rights. The Bill of Rights. No person shall be deprived of life, liberty, or property without due process of law. So ibig sabihin kung madi-deprive ka, kung tatanggal lang ka ng karapatan mo sa kaligtasan mo, kalayaan mo, kayamanan or kaarian mo, lahat yan kailangan sumusunod pa rin sa bata. So a very common example I can give is we are All entitled to liberty. So that means hindi ka pwede basta-basta arestohin. Pero kung aarestohin ka man and you will be deprived of liberty, we have to follow The constitutional guidelines and the law regarding depriving a person of their liberty. Kailangan may warrant of arrest. Tapos pag hinuli ka, bibigay yung rights mo as an accused. Sinusundan yun. So the same thing happens in every other aspect of life where there is a deprivation of life, liberty, or property. Which goes now to my observation na kung allowed naman pala mag-

**[03:09:59.670] Sen. Pia S. Cayetano**  
I can only speak for myself naman.

**[03:10:06.430] Sen. Pia S. Cayetano**  
Kumportable ako na walang labag na batas kasi allowed naman mag-reveal ng bank records in an impeachment trial subject to the guidelines. So that's my number one observation, Mr. President. Bakit ba kailangan unahin yung AMLA? Pero di naman ako yung nagdidikta ng order but I'm presenting it to the body kasi that would have been a more constitutionally

**[03:10:38.630] Sen. Pia S. Cayetano**  
Compliant flow of presentation. Again, that is not my choice, but it is my observation. And I go now to my second point, Mr. President.

**[03:10:54.370] Sen. Pia S. Cayetano**  
Again, on the due process requirement, Section 11 of our AMLA law states that The AMLC may inquire into or examine deposits or investments with any banking institution upon order of any competent court in the cases of violation of this act. So assuming, and that is an assumption, that we accept that this impeachment court is a competent court kasi wala ho yan sa ruling ng ating presiding officer. So I will just make assumptions.

**[03:11:30.780] Sen. Pia S. Cayetano**  
Then, AMLC could present evidence here under Section 11 kasi allowed yan under the law, under the AMLA law sa competent court na sabihin mag-present kayo. But regrettably, Mr. Presiding Officer, months ago, na-present na itong mga AMLA records in the House of Representatives. na hindi naman sila court. I think no less than the prosecution has said during this impeachment trial na hindi sila court.

**[03:12:11.150] Sen. Pia S. Cayetano**  
So, I'm just pointing this out, Your Honor, kasi every time na naki-question yung proceedings natin, nagkakaroon ng duda. Sa mind ng public, kung tama ba ang proceedings natin, kung constitutional ba siya, kung legal siya. At yun lang naman ang gusto nating lahat. **[05:17:12.430] Sen. Francis "Chiz" G. Escudero**  
For the information of the senator-judges, for the information also of counsel-respondent, in case they will be exercising any of the remedies that they are free to exercise, and to enrich the records of the impeachment court as well, allow the chair to read the extended ruling for the record. With your indulgence, Attorney Ronel, I'm sure you've been sitting there for quite some time. And also with your indulgence, Attorney Mae, given the objection of the counsel, the objection of Senator-Judge Aimee, rather.

**[05:17:45.220] Sen. Francis "Chiz" G. Escudero**  
The provision on absolute confidentiality under Section 8 of Republic Act No. 6426 or the Foreign Currency Deposit Act of the Philippines must be read in harmony with Section 9C of Republic Act No. 9160 or the Anti-Money Laundering Act, which expressly requires covert institutions to report covert and suspicious transactions to the AMLC. In making such reports, RA number 9160 provides that covered institutions and their employees shall not be deemed to have violated RA number 6426 as amended. Clearly, the confidentiality afforded to foreign currency deposits does not exempt the latter from the reporting and inquiry provisions under AMLA. Section 76-2 of the BSP's Manual of Regulations on Foreign Exchange Transactions explicitly provides that the absolute confidential nature of foreign currency deposits under the FCDA shall not apply to

**[05:18:52.010] Sen. Francis "Chiz" G. Escudero**  
A covered institution that reports foreign currency deposits in covered transaction reports or suspicious transaction reports to the Anti-Money Laundering Council pursuant to Section 9C of Republic Act No. 9160 as amended. Thus, the suspicious transaction reports or covered transaction reports submitted to the AMLC do not require the status of absolute confidentiality do not acquire, rather, the status of absolute confidentiality merely because they contain information concerning foreign currency deposit accounts. In the aforesighted case of Republic v. Sandiganbayan, the Court ruled that AMLC cannot invoke the confidentiality provisions under the AMLC to prevent disclosure of information on covered and suspicious transactions. The Court did not distinguish between peso and foreign currency denominated transactions. The Court said that unlike covert institutions, the AMLC is mandated to investigate and use the information it has to institute cases against violators. Therefore, the prohibition and confidentiality provisions of AMLA cannot apply to it. Otherwise, it would contravene its direct mandate under Section 7 and unduly hamper criminal prosecution of money laundering activities. This Court also clarifies that it is not at this point compelling the bank or covered institutions to disclose information concerning foreign currency deposits maintained by the respondent. Rather, the witness is simply being asked to testify on records and financial information that are already in his lawful possession through reports submitted by covered institutions to his office. The confidentiality of foreign currency deposits cannot operate to place information lawfully reported to the MLC beyond the reach of the impeachment court. A contrary interpretation would be untenable. While covered institutions are required to flag transactions regardless of currency denominations of the MLC and are at that instance exempt from the confidentiality provisions of the FCDA, the MLC, despite being the government body legally authorized to receive and analyze those reports, would be prevented from explaining or authenticating lawfully acquired information before a competent tribunal. Such an interpretation would substantially impair the statutory functions for which the MLC was created and would run contrary to the Supreme Court's ruling again in Republic v. Sandiganbayan. The aforementioned distinction finds support in the proceedings during the impeachment trial of Chief Justice Renato Corona, where then-Ombudsman Conchita Carpio Morales was presented as a hostile witness by the Respondents' counsel to try and establish that she illegally looked into foreign currency bank accounts of then-Chief Justice Corona. Ombudsman Morales was permitted to testify on information contained in an AMLC report concerning foreign currency bank accounts attributed to the former Chief Justice. In the course of her testimony, it was established that she sourced information from a report submitted by AMLC and not directly from the banks. Asked by then-Senator Miriam Defensor Santiago whether it was the Ombudsman Morales' opinion that the powers of an Ombudsman under the Constitution could override the FCDA, Morales answered that the Anti-Money Laundering Act covers the currency or legal tender of the Philippines or any other country, including dollars, citing Section 3C of Republic Act No. 9160. The confidentiality according to foreign currency deposits has not been treated by the Supreme Court as an infallible rule that admits of no exception. The Court in Salvacion v. Central Bank has allowed the issuance of a writ of execution, for example, on a foreign currency deposit to serve the ends of justice, despite a provision in the FCDA which absolutely exempts foreign currency deposits from attachment, garnishment, order, or process. It further said that allowing the strict application of FCDA to perpetuate injustice would quote, negate Article 10 of the New Civil Code which provides that in case of doubt in the interpretation or application of laws, it is presumed that the lawmaking body intended right and justice to prevail. The Court finally invoked the same principle in China Banking Corporation v. Court of Appeals, GR 140687, when it permitted an inquiry into foreign currency deposits alleged to have been unlawfully taken from their rightful owner, saying that the law and secrecy of foreign currency deposits was not intended by the legislature to perpetuate an injustice. Accordingly, the Court hereby denies the

Number 3, the amount involved is not commensurate with the business or financial capacity of the client. Number 4, taking into account all known circumstances, it may be perceived that the client's transaction is structured in order to avoid being the subject of reporting documents under the Act. Requirements po. Requirements under the Act No. 5, any circumstance relating to the transaction which is observed to deviate from the profile of the client and or the client's past transactions with the covered person.

This prohibition shall apply even after their separation from the AMLC. So based on this provision, the AMLC and its secretariat are required to securely protect confidential information received or possessed by them and prohibits the disclosure of confidential information from the banks and other covered persons.

The punishment of imprisonment ranging from 3 to 8 years and a fine of not less than 500,000 Philippine pesos but not more than 1 million Philippine pesos shall be imposed on a person convicted for a violation under 9C or Section 8A on information security and confidentiality of this Act. If the offender is a public official or employee, he shall, in addition to the penalties prescribed herein, suffer the penalty of perpetual or temporary absolute disqualification from public office, as the case may be. In the case of a breach of confidentiality that is published or reported by media, The responsible reporter, writer, president, publisher, manager, and editor-in-chief shall be liable under this act. After due notice and hearing, the AMLC shall at its discretion impose sanctions including monetary penalties, warning or reprimand upon any covered person, its directors, officers, employees, or any other person for the violation of this Act. It's implementing rules and regulations or for failure or refusal to comply with AMLC orders, resolutions and other issuances. Such monetary penalty shall be in amounts as may be determined by the AMLC to be appropriate, which shall not be more than 500,000 Philippine Pesos per violation.
